[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess the main reason I came in is that I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially in pictures. Since I started having to be more visible at work—like being around people more, helping with events, sometimes getting photographed or seeing myself in group photos—I keep noticing this one part of my face that looks uneven to me.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because other people say they barely notice anything, or they’ll say I look fine, but when I see it, it’s like that’s all I can focus on. I’ll zoom in on photos or compare different angles, and I keep thinking, “How is no one else seeing this?” It feels really obvious to me, even if everyone else acts like it’s nothing.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of… excessive, but it’s been on my mind a lot, and it’s making being in front of people at work feel a lot harder than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face affecting any parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, work is the biggest place I notice it because there are so many event materials and photos and people seeing me, but it kind of spills over into other parts of my life too, or at least it affects how I handle things.\n\nLike, recently I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I got so stuck on how I looked in some event materials. I kept going back and checking the images, trying to figure out if my face looked uneven or if I should ask someone to use a different photo, and then I’d compare it to other versions, and before I knew it I’d spent way too much time on that instead of finishing the actual planning piece I was supposed to do.\n\nAnd now even normal stuff takes me longer than it should. Vendor calls, meeting prep, anything where I might be seen or where my name or picture is connected to the event—I sort of get sidetracked making sure I don’t look… wrong, I guess. So yes, it’s not just that I feel bad about it. It’s actually slowing me down and making me miss things I normally would be on top of.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you think about your face looking uneven?\n\nPatient: It’s… a lot. Like, it’s not just a quick “oh, I don’t like that picture” kind of feeling. When I think about that uneven part of my face, especially if I know other people might see it in a photo or at work, I get really anxious and kind of stuck on it.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Everyone says they barely notice it,” but that doesn’t really calm me down, because to me it feels so obvious. It’s like my attention locks onto that one part and I can’t see the rest of my face normally anymore. I just keep staring at it and thinking, “How can they not see what I’m seeing?” And then I start worrying that maybe people are just being polite or not saying anything.\n\nSo the distress is pretty high, honestly. Not every second of the day, but when it comes up—especially with photos or being in front of people—it can take over my mind way more than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over, like checking your face in the mirror or in photos, in a specific way or a certain number of times to try and feel better about how you look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I want to admit.\n\nBefore I send out event pictures or anything that has my face in it, I’ll check them over and over on my phone. Like I’ll zoom in, then zoom out, then look at it from farther away, then compare it to another picture, and then I’ll open my phone camera just to see if my face looks the same right then. Sometimes I’ll retake a photo a bunch of times, even if it’s just supposed to be something quick, because I’m trying to get an angle where my face doesn’t look so uneven.\n\nAnd before vendor meetings, especially if it’s on video or I might have to talk a lot, I’ll check my face in the camera preview. I’ll tilt my head a certain way, like slightly to one side, because I feel like it hides it better, and then I’ll keep adjusting it to see if that looks normal. It’s not usually a specific number of times, like counting or anything, but it becomes this loop where I don’t feel settled enough to stop.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look weird?” or “Does my face look off in this?” and they’ll say no, but then I still don’t really believe it for long. I might feel okay for a minute, and then I check again.\n\nIt can take up a lot of time. Like enough time that I fall behind on the actual planning work I’m supposed to be doing, because I’m stuck checking how I look instead of just sending the picture or joining the meeting.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face in photos or mirrors, what's the worst thing that you think might happen if you don't get it just right?\n\nPatient: I think the worst thing is that people will see it the way I see it, like that my face looks uneven or strange, and then they’ll judge me or remember me that way. Especially with work stuff, because if it’s an event photo or I’m on a vendor call, I feel like my face is attached to the whole thing I’m responsible for.\n\nSo if I don’t check it enough, I start imagining that I’ll send out a picture where it’s really obvious, or I’ll join a meeting and my camera angle will make it look worse, and everyone will notice but just not say anything. That’s why I keep opening my phone camera, retaking photos, tilting my head a certain way, trying to find the angle where it doesn’t look so off.\n\nAnd then sometimes I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird in this?” because I’m trying to make sure I’m not about to embarrass myself. But even that can turn into this whole thing, because if they say no, I still wonder if they’re just being nice.\n\nIt sounds small when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like if I don’t get it right, I’m going to look really wrong in front of people, and then I can’t focus on the actual planning work. I lose so much time to checking that I end up falling behind on the stuff I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, or could you stop if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: It feels like I have to, yeah. I mean… technically I guess I could stop, like nobody is physically making me do it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel that simple at all.\n\nIf I’m about to send event pictures, I’ll tell myself, “Just send them, they’re fine,” but then I get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I missed something or I’m about to put out a photo where my face looks obviously uneven. So I check it again. I zoom in, I look at the whole photo, I open my phone camera to compare, sometimes I retake it if I can. And if I’m joining a vendor meeting, I’ll keep checking the camera preview and tilting my head a certain way until it looks less off to me.\n\nIt’s like I’m trying to get relief from the anxiety, but the relief doesn’t last. I’ll feel okay for a second, and then I think, “Wait, what if it looks different now?” or “What if that angle is actually worse?” So I do it again. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird, and even if they say no, I don’t fully trust it.\n\nSo could I stop? Maybe if I forced myself, but it would feel really uncomfortable, and I’d be distracted the whole time. Most of the time I end up giving in and checking, and then it eats up enough time that I fall behind on the planning work I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your face looking uneven, is it the flaw itself that bothers you or the idea that others might notice and judge you?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but I think it starts with the flaw itself. Like, I get stuck on this one part of my face that looks uneven to me, especially in photos, and once I see it I can’t really unsee it. I’ll stare at it and compare it to the other side, and it just feels… wrong, like it’s the only thing in the picture.\n\nBut then almost immediately it turns into worrying that other people will notice it too. I came in because since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, that’s gotten so much worse. Like if I’m in event photos or on camera more, I start thinking, “What if everyone can see what I’m seeing?” Even when people tell me they barely notice anything, I don’t really feel reassured for long, because to me it looks obvious.\n\nSo I guess the unevenness itself bothers me a lot, but the part that makes me panic is imagining other people seeing it and judging me for it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face ever led you to avoid social interactions or occupational activities altogether, rather than just being anxious or self-conscious during them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always like I completely don’t show up, but it has made me avoid or delay things in a way that affects work. Like there was part of a planning deadline I basically missed because I got so caught up dealing with how I looked in the event materials. I kept checking the photos and going back and forth on whether my face looked uneven, and I lost time I should’ve been using to finish the actual planning pieces.\n\nAnd with vendor calls or meeting prep, it’s not that I cancel every time, but they take me way longer than they should. I’ll spend extra time setting up the camera, checking the preview, trying different angles, looking at myself before I can even focus on what the call is supposed to be about. So it turns into this whole extra step that other people probably don’t even think about.\n\nSo yes, I guess it has affected occupational stuff more than social stuff. It’s not just feeling self-conscious while I’m doing it — sometimes I put things off or get behind because I’m stuck trying to make sure I don’t look wrong in the materials or on camera.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the uneven part of your face that bothers you, or is it more the general idea that it looks uneven?\n\nPatient: It’s more specific than just a vague “my face looks uneven,” I think. Like… I get pulled into this one area of my face that doesn’t match the other side the way I feel like it should, especially in photos. I don’t know if I’m explaining it right, but once I notice it, it becomes the only thing I can see.\n\nAnd that’s honestly why I came in, because ever since work has put me more in front of people — being in event pictures, being on camera, having my face show up in materials or meetings — I can’t stop staring at that uneven-looking part. I’ll look at a photo and it’s like my eye goes straight there. Even if everyone else says they barely see anything or that I look normal, it doesn’t really feel normal to me in that moment.\n\nSo I guess it’s not just the general idea. It feels like a very specific part looks off, and then my brain kind of builds everything around that.\n\nDoctor: Is the unevenness you see in your face something that you think could be fixed or improved with makeup, surgery, or other means?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it can be “fixed,” exactly. I mean… part of me definitely looks at photos and thinks, if I could just make that one uneven-looking part not stand out, I would feel so much better. Like maybe makeup could help or the right angle or lighting could make it less noticeable.\n\nBut the main thing is that I can’t stop focusing on it. That’s really why I came in. Since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work — photos from events, being on camera, things like that — my eyes go straight to that one part of my face every time. I’ll zoom in or just keep staring, and even if other people say they barely see anything, it doesn’t really register that way to me. It still looks uneven.\n\nSo… I guess I wonder if it could be improved somehow, but I’m not even sure what would actually make me stop noticing it. It feels like I’m always looking for it now.\n\nDoctor: When you think about changing or fixing the uneven part of your face, is it more about making it look perfect to yourself or about avoiding what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it starts with me seeing it and not being able to unsee it. Like, I came in because ever since I’ve had to be more visible at work — in event photos, on camera, in things where my face is just there for people to look at — I can’t stop staring at this one uneven-looking part of my face in photos.\n\nIt’s not even like someone has said something mean about it. Most people, if I ask or if it comes up, say they barely see anything, or they’ll say I look fine. But that doesn’t really calm it down for me, because I’ll look at the picture and my eye goes straight there, and then I’m thinking, how can they not see that? So I guess it’s about wanting it to look right to me, but then because it looks so obvious to me, I start assuming other people must be noticing it too, even if they say they aren’t.\n\nSo… I don’t know if “perfect” is the word, but I want it to stop looking wrong to me. And then maybe I wouldn’t worry so much about what other people think.\n\nDoctor: When you're worrying about your face looking uneven, is it more that you're afraid of what others will think of you, or that you genuinely believe it looks flawed?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I genuinely believe it looks flawed, at least in the moment when I’m looking at it. The worry about other people comes after, because if it looks that obvious to me, then I start thinking, well, how could they not notice it too?\n\nBut the main thing is… ever since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, like being in event photos or on camera more, I just can’t stop staring at this one uneven-looking part of my face in pictures. My eye goes straight to it every time. I’ll try to look at the whole photo like a normal person, but I end up focusing on that one area and it feels like it ruins the whole thing.\n\nAnd what makes it confusing is that other people will say they barely see anything, or they’ll tell me it looks fine, but that doesn’t really match what I’m seeing. So it’s not only, “I’m scared they’ll judge me.” It’s more like I’m looking at it and thinking, “No, this really looks uneven,” and then I get scared other people are thinking the same thing.\n\nDoctor: When you're worrying about your face, is it more about the unevenness itself or the possibility that others might see it and think less of you?\n\nPatient: It’s more about the unevenness itself, I think. Like, the other-people part is there, obviously, because I’m in front of people more at work now and there are photos from events and things where I know people can see my face. But it starts with me looking at it and feeling like that one part just looks wrong or uneven.\n\nI’ll see a photo and I can’t really take in the whole picture. My eyes go straight to that spot, and then I’ll keep staring at it, like I’m trying to figure out if it’s as bad as it looks or if it’s just the angle. And even when people tell me they barely see anything, it doesn’t really help much, because I still see it. It’s like I can’t unsee it once I notice it.\n\nSo I guess I do worry that other people might notice and think something, but the main thing is that to me it already looks noticeable. That’s what I get stuck on.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about your face, is it the fear of what others might think that's driving your concern, or is it more about your own perception of how you look?\n\nPatient: It’s more about my own perception, I think. I mean, I do worry about what people might think, especially because my job has made me more visible — like being in event photos, or on camera, or just having my face out there more than before. I lose so much time to checking that I end up falling behind on the stuff I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, or could you stop if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: It feels like I have to, yeah. I mean… technically I guess I could stop, like nobody is physically making me do it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel that simple at all.\n\nIf I’m about to send event pictures, I’ll tell myself, “Just send them, they’re fine,” but then I get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I missed something or I’m about to put out a photo where my face looks obviously uneven. Like, the other-people part is there, obviously, because I’m in front of people more at work now and there are photos from events and things where I know people can see my face. But it starts with me looking at it and feeling like that one part just looks wrong or uneven.\n\nI’ll see a photo and I can’t really take in the whole picture. My eyes go straight to that spot, and then I’ll keep staring at it, like I’m trying to figure out if it’s as bad as it looks or if it’s just the angle. And even when people tell me they barely see anything, it doesn’t really help much, because I still see it. It’s like I can’t unsee it once I notice it.\n\nSo I guess I do worry that other people might notice and think something, but the main thing is that to me it already looks noticeable. That’s what I get stuck on.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about your face, is it the fear of what others might think that's driving your concern, or is it more about your own perception of how you look?\n\nPatient: It’s more about my own perception, I think. I mean, I do worry about what people might think, especially because my job has made me more visible — like being in event photos, or on camera, or just having my face out there more than before. That’s definitely made it worse.\n\nBut the thing that really starts it is me seeing it. I’ll look at a photo and it’s like I can’t just see my face as a whole. I go straight to this one uneven-looking part, and then I keep staring at it, almost like I’m checking whether it really looks as off as I think it does. And even if other people say they barely notice anything, or that it looks fine, I still see it right away.\n\nSo it’s not only that I’m scared people will judge me. It’s more like… to me, it already looks wrong, and then I assume other people must be seeing what I’m seeing. That’s why I came in, because I can’t seem to stop focusing on it.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're worried about your face, is it the fact that you think it looks uneven that bothers you, or is it the thought that others might notice and judge you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / meeting_convention_or_event_planner / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I came in because ever since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I can’t stop staring at this one uneven-looking part of my face in photos, even when everyone else says they barely see anything.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mostly the fact that I think it looks uneven. The judging part is there, but it comes after, if that makes sense.

Like, the reason I came in is because ever since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work — in event photos, or just being seen more — I can’t stop looking at this one part of my face in pictures. It’s like my eyes go straight there before I even have a chance to look at the rest of the photo. And then I keep zooming in or staring at it, trying to decide if it’s really that uneven or if maybe it’s just the lighting or the angle, but it still looks wrong to me.

And people will say, “I barely see what you mean,” or “You look fine,” but that doesn’t really settle it in my head, because I’m still seeing it so clearly. So yes, I worry other people might notice and judge me, especially because of work, but the part that bothers me first is that I genuinely feel like it looks uneven.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess the main reason I came in is that I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially in pictures. Since I started having to be more visible at work—like being around people more, helping with events, sometimes getting photographed or seeing myself in group photos—I keep noticing this one part of my face that looks uneven to me.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because other people say they barely notice anything, or they’ll say I look fine, but when I see it, it’s like that’s all I can focus on. I’ll zoom in on photos or compare different angles, and I keep thinking, “How is no one else seeing this?” It feels really obvious to me, even if everyone else acts like it’s nothing.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of… excessive, but it’s been on my mind a lot, and it’s making being in front of people at work feel a lot harder than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face affecting any parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, work is the biggest place I notice it because there are so many event materials and photos and people seeing me, but it kind of spills over into other parts of my life too, or at least it affects how I handle things.\n\nLike, recently I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I got so stuck on how I looked in some event materials. I kept going back and checking the images, trying to figure out if my face looked uneven or if I should ask someone to use a different photo, and then I’d compare it to other versions, and before I knew it I’d spent way too much time on that instead of finishing the actual planning piece I was supposed to do.\n\nAnd now even normal stuff takes me longer than it should. Vendor calls, meeting prep, anything where I might be seen or where my name or picture is connected to the event—I sort of get sidetracked making sure I don’t look… wrong, I guess. So yes, it’s not just that I feel bad about it. It’s actually slowing me down and making me miss things I normally would be on top of.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you think about your face looking uneven?\n\nPatient: It’s… a lot. Like, it’s not just a quick “oh, I don’t like that picture” kind of feeling. It’s like my attention locks onto that one part and I can’t see the rest of my face normally anymore. I just keep staring at it and thinking, “How can they not see what I’m seeing?” And then I start worrying that maybe people are just being polite or not saying anything.\n\nSo the distress is pretty high, honestly. Not every second of the day, but when it comes up—especially with photos or being in front of people—it can take over my mind way more than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over, like checking your face in the mirror or in photos, in a specific way or a certain number of times to try and feel better about how you look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I want to admit.\n\nBefore I send out event pictures or anything that has my face in it, I’ll check them over and over on my phone. Like I’ll zoom in, then zoom out, then look at it from farther away, then compare it to another picture, and then I’ll open my phone camera just to see if my face looks the same right then. Sometimes I’ll retake a photo a bunch of times, even if it’s just supposed to be something quick, because I’m trying to get an angle where my face doesn’t look so uneven.\n\nAnd before vendor meetings, especially if it’s on video or I might have to talk a lot, I’ll check my face in the camera preview. I’ll tilt my head a certain way, like slightly to one side, because I feel like it hides it better, and then I’ll keep adjusting it to see if that looks normal. It’s not usually a specific number of times, like counting or anything, but it becomes this loop where I don’t feel settled enough to stop.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look weird?” or “Does my face look off in this?” and they’ll say no, but then I still don’t really believe it for long. I might feel okay for a minute, and then I check again.\n\nIt can take up a lot of time. Like enough time that I fall behind on the actual planning work I’m supposed to be doing, because I’m stuck checking how I look instead of just sending the picture or joining the meeting.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face in photos or mirrors, what's the worst thing that you think might happen if you don't get it just right?\n\nPatient: I think the worst thing is that people will see it the way I see it, like that my face looks uneven or strange, and then they’ll judge me or remember me that way. Especially with work stuff, because if it’s an event photo or I’m on a vendor call, I feel like my face is attached to the whole thing I’m responsible for.\n\nSo if I don’t check it enough, I start imagining that I’ll send out a picture where it’s really obvious, or I’ll join a meeting and my camera angle will make it look worse, and everyone will notice but just not say anything. That’s why I keep opening my phone camera, retaking photos, tilting my head a certain way, trying to find the angle where it doesn’t look so off.\n\nAnd then sometimes I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird in this?” because I’m trying to make sure I’m not about to embarrass myself. But even that can turn into this whole thing, because if they say no, I still wonder if they’re just being nice.\n\nIt sounds small when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like if I don’t get it right, I’m going to look really wrong in front of people, and then I can’t focus on the actual planning work. I lose so much time to checking that I end up falling behind on the stuff I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, or could you stop if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: It feels like I have to, yeah. I mean… technically I guess I could stop, like nobody is physically making me do it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel that simple at all.\n\nIf I’m about to send event pictures, I’ll tell myself, “Just send them, they’re fine,” but then I get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I missed something or I’m about to put out a photo where my face looks obviously uneven. So I check it again. I zoom in, I look at the whole photo, I open my phone camera to compare, sometimes I retake it if I can. And if I’m joining a vendor meeting, I’ll keep checking the camera preview and tilting my head a certain way until it looks less off to me.\n\nIt’s like I’m trying to get relief from the anxiety, but the relief doesn’t last. I’ll feel okay for a second, and then I think, “Wait, what if it looks different now?” or “What if that angle is actually worse?” So I do it again. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird, and even if they say no, I don’t fully trust it.\n\nSo could I stop? Maybe if I forced myself, but it would feel really uncomfortable, and I’d be distracted the whole time. Most of the time I end up giving in and checking, and then it eats up enough time that I fall behind on the planning work I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your face looking uneven, is it the flaw itself that bothers you or the idea that others might notice and judge you?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but I think it starts with the flaw itself. Like, I get stuck on this one part of my face that looks uneven to me, especially in photos, and once I see it I can’t really unsee it. I’ll stare at it and compare it to the other side, and it just feels… wrong, like it’s the only thing in the picture.\n\nBut then almost immediately it turns into worrying that other people will notice it too. I came in because since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, that’s gotten so much worse. Like if I’m in event photos or on camera more, I start thinking, “What if everyone can see what I’m seeing?” Even when people tell me they barely notice anything, I don’t really feel reassured for long, because to me it looks obvious.\n\nSo I guess the unevenness itself bothers me a lot, but the part that makes me panic is imagining other people seeing it and judging me for it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face ever led you to avoid social interactions or occupational activities altogether, rather than just being anxious or self-conscious during them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always like I completely don’t show up, but it has made me avoid or delay things in a way that affects work. Like there was part of a planning deadline I basically missed because I got so caught up dealing with how I looked in the event materials. I kept checking the photos and going back and forth on whether my face looked uneven, and I lost time I should’ve been using to finish the actual planning pieces.\n\nAnd with vendor calls or meeting prep, it’s not that I cancel every time, but they take me way longer than they should. I’ll spend extra time setting up the camera, checking the preview, trying different angles, looking at myself before I can even focus on what the call is supposed to be about. So it turns into this whole extra step that other people probably don’t even think about.\n\nSo yes, I guess it has affected occupational stuff more than social stuff. It’s not just feeling self-conscious while I’m doing it — sometimes I put things off or get behind because I’m stuck trying to make sure I don’t look wrong in the materials or on camera.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the uneven part of your face that bothers you, or is it more the general idea that it looks uneven?\n\nPatient: It’s more specific than just a vague “my face looks uneven,” I think. Like… I get pulled into this one area of my face that doesn’t match the other side the way I feel like it should, especially in photos. I don’t know if I’m explaining it right, but once I notice it, it becomes the only thing I can see.\n\nAnd that’s honestly why I came in, because ever since work has put me more in front of people — being in event pictures, being on camera, having my face show up in materials or meetings — I can’t stop staring at that uneven-looking part. I’ll look at a photo and it’s like my eye goes straight there. Even if everyone else says they barely see anything or that I look normal, it doesn’t really feel normal to me in that moment.\n\nSo I guess it’s not just the general idea. It feels like a very specific part looks off, and then my brain kind of builds everything around that.\n\nDoctor: Is the unevenness you see in your face something that you think could be fixed or improved with makeup, surgery, or other means?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it can be “fixed,” exactly. I mean… part of me definitely looks at photos and thinks, if I could just make that one uneven-looking part not stand out, I would feel so much better. Like maybe makeup could help or the right angle or lighting could make it less noticeable.\n\nBut the main thing is that I can’t stop focusing on it. That’s really why I came in. Since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work — photos from events, being on camera, things like that — my eyes go straight to that one part of my face every time. I’ll zoom in or just keep staring, and even if other people say they barely see anything, it doesn’t really register that way to me. It still looks uneven.\n\nSo… I guess I wonder if it could be improved somehow, but I’m not even sure what would actually make me stop noticing it. It feels like I’m always looking for it now.\n\nDoctor: When you think about changing or fixing the uneven part of your face, is it more about making it look perfect to yourself or about avoiding what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it starts with me seeing it and not being able to unsee it. Like, I came in because ever since I’ve had to be more visible at work — in event photos, on camera, in things where my face is just there for people to look at — I can’t stop staring at this one uneven-looking part of my face in photos.\n\nIt’s not even like someone has said something mean about it. Most people, if I ask or if it comes up, say they barely see anything, or they’ll say I look fine. But that doesn’t really calm it down for me, because I’ll look at the picture and my eye goes straight there, and then I’m thinking, how can they not see that? So I guess it’s about wanting it to look right to me, but then because it looks so obvious to me, I start assuming other people must be noticing it too, even if they say they aren’t.\n\nSo… I don’t know if “perfect” is the word, but I want it to stop looking wrong to me. And then maybe I wouldn’t worry so much about what other people think.\n\nDoctor: When you're worrying about your face looking uneven, is it more that you're afraid of what others will think of you, or that you genuinely believe it looks flawed?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I genuinely believe it looks flawed, at least in the moment when I’m looking at it. The worry about other people comes after, because if it looks that obvious to me, then I start thinking, well, how could they not notice it too?\n\nBut the main thing is… ever since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, like being in event photos or on camera more, I just can’t stop staring at this one uneven-looking part of my face in pictures. My eye goes straight to it every time. I’ll try to look at the whole photo like a normal person, but I end up focusing on that one area and it feels like it ruins the whole thing.\n\nAnd what makes it confusing is that other people will say they barely see anything, or they’ll tell me it looks fine, but that doesn’t really match what I’m seeing. So it’s not only, “I’m scared they’ll judge me.” It’s more like I’m looking at it and thinking, “No, this really looks uneven,” and then I get scared other people are thinking the same thing.\n\nDoctor: When you're worrying about your face, is it more about the unevenness itself or the possibility that others might see it and think less of you?\n\nPatient: It’s more about the unevenness itself, I think. Especially with work stuff, because if it’s an event photo or I’m on a vendor call, I feel like my face is attached to the whole thing I’m responsible for.\n\nSo if I don’t check it enough, I start imagining that I’ll send out a picture where it’s really obvious, or I’ll join a meeting and my camera angle will make it look worse, and everyone will notice but just not say anything. That’s why I keep opening my phone camera, retaking photos, tilting my head a certain way, trying to find the angle where it doesn’t look so off.\n\nAnd then sometimes I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird in this?” because I’m trying to make sure I’m not about to embarrass myself. But even that can turn into this whole thing, because if they say no, I still wonder if they’re just being nice.\n\nIt sounds small when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like if I don’t get it right, I’m going to look really wrong in front of people, and then I can’t focus on the actual planning work. I lose so much time to checking that I end up falling behind on the stuff I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, or could you stop if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: It feels like I have to, yeah. I mean… technically I guess I could stop, like nobody is physically making me do it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel that simple at all.\n\nIf I’m about to send event pictures, I’ll tell myself, “Just send them, they’re fine,” but then I get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I missed something or I’m about to put out a photo where my face looks obviously uneven. So I check it again. I zoom in, I look at the whole photo, I open my phone camera to compare, sometimes I retake it if I can. And if I’m joining a vendor meeting, I’ll keep checking the camera preview and tilting my head a certain way until it looks less off to me.\n\nIt’s like I’m trying to get relief from the anxiety, but the relief doesn’t last. I’ll feel okay for a second, and then I think, “Wait, what if it looks different now?” or “What if that angle is actually worse?” So I do it again. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird, and even if they say no, I don’t fully trust it.\n\nSo could I stop? Maybe if I forced myself, but it would feel really uncomfortable, and I’d be distracted the whole time. Most of the time I end up giving in and checking, and then it eats up enough time that I fall behind on the planning work I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your face looking uneven, is it the flaw itself that bothers you or the idea that others might notice and judge you?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but I think it starts with the flaw itself. Like, I get stuck on this one part of my face that looks uneven to me, especially in photos, and once I see it I can’t really unsee it. I’ll stare at it and compare it to the other side, and it just feels… wrong, like it’s the only thing in the picture.\n\nBut then almost immediately it turns into worrying that other people will notice it too. I came in because since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, that’s gotten so much worse. Like if I’m in event photos or on camera more, I start thinking, “What if everyone can see what I’m seeing?” Even when people tell me they barely notice anything, I don’t really feel reassured for long, because to me it looks obvious.\n\nSo I guess the unevenness itself bothers me a lot, but the part that makes me panic is imagining other people seeing it and judging me for it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face ever led you to avoid social interactions or occupational activities altogether, rather than just being anxious or self-conscious during them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always like I completely don’t show up, but it has made me avoid or delay things in a way that affects work. Like there was part of a planning deadline I basically missed because I got so caught up dealing with how I looked in the event materials. I kept checking the photos and going back and forth on whether my face looked uneven, and I lost time I should’ve been using to finish the actual planning pieces.\n\nAnd with vendor calls or meeting prep, it’s not that I cancel every time, but they take me way longer than they should. I’ll spend extra time setting up the camera, checking the preview, trying different angles, looking at myself before I can even focus on what the call is supposed to be about. So it turns into this whole extra step that other people probably don’t even think about.\n\nSo yes, I guess it has affected occupational stuff more than social stuff. It’s not just feeling self-conscious while I’m doing it — sometimes I put things off or get behind because I’m stuck trying to make sure I don’t look wrong in the materials or on camera.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the uneven part of your face that bothers you, or is it more the general idea that it looks uneven?\n\nPatient: It’s more specific than just a vague “my face looks uneven,” I think. Like… I get pulled into this one area of my face that doesn’t match the other side the way I feel like it should, especially in photos. I don’t know if I’m explaining it right, but once I notice it, it becomes the only thing I can see.\n\nAnd that’s honestly why I came in, because ever since work has put me more in front of people — being in event pictures, being on camera, having my face show up in materials or meetings — I can’t stop staring at that uneven-looking part. I’ll look at a photo and it’s like my eye goes straight there. Even if everyone else says they barely see anything or that I look normal, it doesn’t really feel normal to me in that moment.\n\nSo I guess it’s not just the general idea. It feels like a very specific part looks off, and then my brain kind of builds everything around that.\n\nDoctor: Is the unevenness you see in your face something that you think could be fixed or improved with makeup, surgery, or other means?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it can be “fixed,” exactly. I mean… part of me definitely looks at photos and thinks, if I could just make that one uneven-looking part not stand out, I would feel so much better. Like maybe makeup could help or the right angle or lighting could make it less noticeable.\n\nBut the main thing is that I can’t stop focusing on it. That’s really why I came in. Since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work — photos from events, being on camera, things like that — my eyes go straight to that one part of my face every time. I’ll zoom in or just keep staring, and even if other people say they barely see anything, it doesn’t really register that way to me. It still looks uneven.\n\nSo… I guess I wonder if it could be improved somehow, but I’m not even sure what would actually make me stop noticing it. It feels like I’m always looking for it now.\n\nDoctor: When you think about changing or fixing the uneven part of your face, is it more about making it look perfect to yourself or about avoiding what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it starts with me seeing it and not being able to unsee it. Like, I came in because ever since I’ve had to be more visible at work — in event photos, on camera, in things where my face is just there for people to look at — I can’t stop staring at this one uneven-looking part of my face in photos.\n\nIt’s not even like someone has said something mean about it. Most people, if I ask or if it comes up, say they barely see anything, or they’ll say I look fine. But that doesn’t really calm it down for me, because I’ll look at the picture and my eye goes straight there, and then I’m thinking, how can they not see that? So I guess it’s about wanting it to look right to me, but then because it looks so obvious to me, I start assuming other people must be noticing it too, even if they say they aren’t.\n\nSo… I don’t know if “perfect” is the word, but I want it to stop looking wrong to me. And then maybe I wouldn’t worry so much about what other people think.\n\nDoctor: When you're worrying about your face looking uneven, is it more that you're afraid of what others will think of you, or that you genuinely believe it looks flawed?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I genuinely believe it looks flawed, at least in the moment when I’m looking at it. The worry about other people comes after, because if it looks that obvious to me, then I start thinking, well, how could they not notice it too?\n\nBut the main thing is… ever since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, like being in event photos or on camera more, I just can’t stop staring at this one uneven-looking part of my face in pictures. My eye goes straight to it every time. I’ll try to look at the whole photo like a normal person, but I end up focusing on that one area and it feels like it ruins the whole thing.\n\nAnd what makes it confusing is that other people will say they barely see anything, or they’ll tell me it looks fine, but that doesn’t really match what I’m seeing. So it’s not only, “I’m scared they’ll judge me.” It’s more like I’m looking at it and thinking, “No, this really looks uneven,” and then I get scared other people are thinking the same thing.\n\nDoctor: When you're worrying about your face, is it more about the unevenness itself or the possibility that others might see it and think less of you?\n\nPatient: It’s more about the unevenness itself, I think. Like, the other-people part is there, obviously, because I’m in front of people more at work now and there are photos from events and things where I know people can see my face. But it starts with me looking at it and feeling like that one part just looks wrong or uneven.\n\nI’ll see a photo and I can’t really take in the whole picture. My eyes go straight to that spot, and then I’ll keep staring at it, like I’m trying to figure out if it’s as bad as it looks or if it’s just the angle. And even when people tell me they barely see anything, it doesn’t really help much, because I still see it. It’s like I can’t unsee it once I notice it.\n\nSo I guess I do worry that other people might notice and think something, but the main thing is that to me it already looks noticeable. That’s what I get stuck on.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about your face, is it the fear of what others might think that's driving your concern, or is it more about your own perception of how you look?\n\nPatient: It’s more about my own perception, I think. I mean, I do worry about what people might think, especially because my job has made me more visible — like being in event photos, or on camera, or just having my face out there more than before. That’s definitely made it worse.\n\nBut the thing that really starts it is me seeing it. I’ll look at a photo and it’s like I can’t just see my face as a whole. I go straight to this one uneven-looking part, and then I keep staring at it, almost like I’m checking whether it really looks as off as I think it does. And even if other people say they barely notice anything, or that it looks fine, I still see it right away.\n\nSo it’s not only that I’m scared people will judge me. It’s more like… to me, it already looks wrong, and then I assume other people must be seeing what I’m seeing. That’s why I came in, because I can’t seem to stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about your face, is it the fact that you think it looks uneven that bothers you, or is it the thought that others might notice and judge you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the fact that I think it looks uneven. The judging part is there, but it comes after, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, the reason I came in is because ever since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work — in event photos, or just being seen more — I can’t stop looking at this one part of my face in pictures. It’s like my eyes go straight there before I even have a chance to look at the rest of the photo. And then I keep zooming in or staring at it, trying to decide if it’s really that uneven or if maybe it’s just the lighting or the angle, but it still looks wrong to me.\n\nAnd people will say, “I barely see what you mean,” or “You look fine,” but that doesn’t really settle it in my head, because I’m still seeing it so clearly. It’s more that I genuinely believe something looks wrong, honestly. I know the being judged part is wrapped up in it, especially because of work — like, I’m more in front of people now, there are event photos, people taking pictures, sometimes posting them, and I can’t really avoid seeing my face as much as I used to.

But the thing that gets me stuck is this one uneven-looking part of my face. I’ll see a photo and I don’t even really notice the rest of it first. I go straight to that area, and then I’ll keep staring at it, like I’m trying to figure out if it’s actually uneven or if maybe it’s just the angle or lighting. And it doesn’t really matter if everyone else says they barely see anything, because I still see it right away.

So yes, I do worry people might judge me, but it’s not like that’s the whole reason. It’s more like I already feel convinced it looks off, and then I think, well, if I can see it this clearly, other people probably can too.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess the main reason I came in is that I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially in pictures. Since I started having to be more visible at work—like being around people more, helping with events, sometimes getting photographed or seeing myself in group photos—I keep noticing this one part of my face that looks uneven to me.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because other people say they barely notice anything, or they’ll say I look fine, but when I see it, it’s like that’s all I can focus on. I’ll zoom in on photos or compare different angles, and I keep thinking, “How is no one else seeing this?” It feels really obvious to me, even if everyone else acts like it’s nothing.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of… excessive, but it’s been on my mind a lot, and it’s making being in front of people at work feel a lot harder than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face affecting any parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, work is the biggest place I notice it because there are so many event materials and photos and people seeing me, but it kind of spills over into other parts of my life too, or at least it affects how I handle things.\n\nLike, recently I actually missed part of a planning deadline because I got so stuck on how I looked in some event materials. I kept going back and checking the images, trying to figure out if my face looked uneven or if I should ask someone to use a different photo, and then I’d compare it to other versions, and before I knew it I’d spent way too much time on that instead of finishing the actual planning piece I was supposed to do.\n\nAnd now even normal stuff takes me longer than it should. Vendor calls, meeting prep, anything where I might be seen or where my name or picture is connected to the event—I sort of get sidetracked making sure I don’t look… wrong, I guess. So yes, it’s not just that I feel bad about it. It’s actually slowing me down and making me miss things I normally would be on top of.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety do you feel when you think about your face looking uneven?\n\nPatient: It’s… a lot. Like, it’s not just a quick “oh, I don’t like that picture” kind of feeling. When I think about that uneven part of my face, especially if I know other people might see it in a photo or at work, I get really anxious and kind of stuck on it.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Everyone says they barely notice it,” but that doesn’t really calm me down, because to me it feels so obvious. It’s like my attention locks onto that one part and I can’t see the rest of my face normally anymore. I just keep staring at it and thinking, “How can they not see what I’m seeing?” And then I start worrying that maybe people are just being polite or not saying anything.\n\nSo the distress is pretty high, honestly. Not every second of the day, but when it comes up—especially with photos or being in front of people—it can take over my mind way more than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over, like checking your face in the mirror or in photos, in a specific way or a certain number of times to try and feel better about how you look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I want to admit.\n\nBefore I send out event pictures or anything that has my face in it, I’ll check them over and over on my phone. Like I’ll zoom in, then zoom out, then look at it from farther away, then compare it to another picture, and then I’ll open my phone camera just to see if my face looks the same right then. Sometimes I’ll retake a photo a bunch of times, even if it’s just supposed to be something quick, because I’m trying to get an angle where my face doesn’t look so uneven.\n\nAnd before vendor meetings, especially if it’s on video or I might have to talk a lot, I’ll check my face in the camera preview. I’ll tilt my head a certain way, like slightly to one side, because I feel like it hides it better, and then I’ll keep adjusting it to see if that looks normal. It’s not usually a specific number of times, like counting or anything, but it becomes this loop where I don’t feel settled enough to stop.\n\nSometimes I’ll ask a coworker, “Do I look weird?” or “Does my face look off in this?” and they’ll say no, but then I still don’t really believe it for long. I might feel okay for a minute, and then I check again.\n\nIt can take up a lot of time. Like enough time that I fall behind on the actual planning work I’m supposed to be doing, because I’m stuck checking how I look instead of just sending the picture or joining the meeting.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face in photos or mirrors, what's the worst thing that you think might happen if you don't get it just right?\n\nPatient: I think the worst thing is that people will see it the way I see it, like that my face looks uneven or strange, and then they’ll judge me or remember me that way. Especially with work stuff, because if it’s an event photo or I’m on a vendor call, I feel like my face is attached to the whole thing I’m responsible for.\n\nSo if I don’t check it enough, I start imagining that I’ll send out a picture where it’s really obvious, or I’ll join a meeting and my camera angle will make it look worse, and everyone will notice but just not say anything. That’s why I keep opening my phone camera, retaking photos, tilting my head a certain way, trying to find the angle where it doesn’t look so off.\n\nAnd then sometimes I’ll ask a coworker, like, “Do I look weird in this?” because I’m trying to make sure I’m not about to embarrass myself. But even that can turn into this whole thing, because if they say no, I still wonder if they’re just being nice.\n\nIt sounds small when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels like if I don’t get it right, I’m going to look really wrong in front of people, and then I can’t focus on the actual planning work. I lose so much time to checking that I end up falling behind on the stuff I was supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, or could you stop if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: It feels like I have to, yeah. I mean… technically I guess I could stop, like nobody is physically making me do it, but in the moment it doesn’t feel that simple at all.\n\nIf I’m about to send event pictures, I’ll tell myself, “Just send them, they’re fine,” but then I get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I missed something or I’m about to put out a photo where my face looks obviously uneven. So I check it again. I zoom in, I look at the whole photo, I open my phone camera to compare, sometimes I retake it if I can. And if I’m joining a vendor meeting, I’ll keep checking the camera preview and tilting my head a certain way until it looks less off to me.\n\nIt’s like I’m trying to get relief from the anxiety, but the relief doesn’t last. I’ll feel okay for a second, and then I think, “Wait, what if it looks different now?” or “What if that angle is actually worse?” So I do it again. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird, and even if they say no, I don’t fully trust it.\n\nSo could I stop? Maybe if I forced myself, but it would feel really uncomfortable, and I’d be distracted the whole time. Most of the time I end up giving in and checking, and then it eats up enough time that I fall behind on the planning work I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your face looking uneven, is it the flaw itself that bothers you or the idea that others might notice and judge you?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but I think it starts with the flaw itself. Like, I get stuck on this one part of my face that looks uneven to me, especially in photos, and once I see it I can’t really unsee it. I’ll stare at it and compare it to the other side, and it just feels… wrong, like it’s the only thing in the picture.\n\nBut then almost immediately it turns into worrying that other people will notice it too. I came in because since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, that’s gotten so much worse. Like if I’m in event photos or on camera more, I start thinking, “What if everyone can see what I’m seeing?” Even when people tell me they barely notice anything, I don’t really feel reassured for long, because to me it looks obvious.\n\nSo I guess the unevenness itself bothers me a lot, but the part that makes me panic is imagining other people seeing it and judging me for it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face ever led you to avoid social interactions or occupational activities altogether, rather than just being anxious or self-conscious during them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not always like I completely don’t show up, but it has made me avoid or delay things in a way that affects work. Like there was part of a planning deadline I basically missed because I got so caught up dealing with how I looked in the event materials. I kept checking the photos and going back and forth on whether my face looked uneven, and I lost time I should’ve been using to finish the actual planning pieces.\n\nAnd with vendor calls or meeting prep, it’s not that I cancel every time, but they take me way longer than they should. I’ll spend extra time setting up the camera, checking the preview, trying different angles, looking at myself before I can even focus on what the call is supposed to be about. So it turns into this whole extra step that other people probably don’t even think about.\n\nSo yes, I guess it has affected occupational stuff more than social stuff. It’s not just feeling self-conscious while I’m doing it — sometimes I put things off or get behind because I’m stuck trying to make sure I don’t look wrong in the materials or on camera.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the uneven part of your face that bothers you, or is it more the general idea that it looks uneven?\n\nPatient: It’s more specific than just a vague “my face looks uneven,” I think. Like… I get pulled into this one area of my face that doesn’t match the other side the way I feel like it should, especially in photos. I don’t know if I’m explaining it right, but once I notice it, it becomes the only thing I can see.\n\nAnd that’s honestly why I came in, because ever since work has put me more in front of people — being in event pictures, being on camera, having my face show up in materials or meetings — I can’t stop staring at that uneven-looking part. I’ll look at a photo and it’s like my eye goes straight there. Even if everyone else says they barely see anything or that I look normal, it doesn’t really feel normal to me in that moment.\n\nSo I guess it’s not just the general idea. It feels like a very specific part looks off, and then my brain kind of builds everything around that.\n\nDoctor: Is the unevenness you see in your face something that you think could be fixed or improved with makeup, surgery, or other means?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it can be “fixed,” exactly. I mean… part of me definitely looks at photos and thinks, if I could just make that one uneven-looking part not stand out, I would feel so much better. Like maybe makeup could help or the right angle or lighting could make it less noticeable.\n\nBut the main thing is that I can’t stop focusing on it. That’s really why I came in. Since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work — photos from events, being on camera, things like that — my eyes go straight to that one part of my face every time. I’ll zoom in or just keep staring, and even if other people say they barely see anything, it doesn’t really register that way to me. It still looks uneven.\n\nSo… I guess I wonder if it could be improved somehow, but I’m not even sure what would actually make me stop noticing it. It feels like I’m always looking for it now.\n\nDoctor: When you think about changing or fixing the uneven part of your face, is it more about making it look perfect to yourself or about avoiding what others might think of you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m being honest, it starts with me seeing it and not being able to unsee it. Like, I came in because ever since I’ve had to be more visible at work — in event photos, on camera, in things where my face is just there for people to look at — I can’t stop staring at this one uneven-looking part of my face in photos.\n\nIt’s not even like someone has said something mean about it. Most people, if I ask or if it comes up, say they barely see anything, or they’ll say I look fine. But that doesn’t really calm it down for me, because I’ll look at the picture and my eye goes straight there, and then I’m thinking, how can they not see that? So I guess it’s about wanting it to look right to me, but then because it looks so obvious to me, I start assuming other people must be noticing it too, even if they say they aren’t.\n\nSo… I don’t know if “perfect” is the word, but I want it to stop looking wrong to me. And then maybe I wouldn’t worry so much about what other people think.\n\nDoctor: When you're worrying about your face looking uneven, is it more that you're afraid of what others will think of you, or that you genuinely believe it looks flawed?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I genuinely believe it looks flawed, at least in the moment when I’m looking at it. The worry about other people comes after, because if it looks that obvious to me, then I start thinking, well, how could they not notice it too?\n\nBut the main thing is… ever since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, like being in event photos or on camera more, I just can’t stop staring at this one uneven-looking part of my face in pictures. My eye goes straight to it every time. I’ll try to look at the whole photo like a normal person, but I end up focusing on that one area and it feels like it ruins the whole thing.\n\nAnd what makes it confusing is that other people will say they barely see anything, or they’ll tell me it looks fine, but that doesn’t really match what I’m seeing. So it’s not only, “I’m scared they’ll judge me.” It’s more like I’m looking at it and thinking, “No, this really looks uneven,” and then I get scared other people are thinking the same thing.\n\nDoctor: When you're worrying about your face, is it more about the unevenness itself or the possibility that others might see it and think less of you?\n\nPatient: It’s more about the unevenness itself, I think. Like, the other-people part is there, obviously, because I’m in front of people more at work now and there are photos from events and things where I know people can see my face. But it starts with me looking at it and feeling like that one part just looks wrong or uneven.\n\nI’ll see a photo and I can’t really take in the whole picture. My eyes go straight to that spot, and then I’ll keep staring at it, like I’m trying to figure out if it’s as bad as it looks or if it’s just the angle. And even when people tell me they barely see anything, it doesn’t really help much, because I still see it. It’s like I can’t unsee it once I notice it.\n\nSo I guess I do worry that other people might notice and think something, but the main thing is that to me it already looks noticeable. That’s what I get stuck on.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about your face, is it the fear of what others might think that's driving your concern, or is it more about your own perception of how you look?\n\nPatient: It’s more about my own perception, I think. I mean, I do worry about what people might think, especially because my job has made me more visible — like being in event photos, or on camera, or just having my face out there more than before. That’s definitely made it worse.\n\nBut the thing that really starts it is me seeing it. I’ll look at a photo and it’s like I can’t just see my face as a whole. I go straight to this one uneven-looking part, and then I keep staring at it, almost like I’m checking whether it really looks as off as I think it does. And even if other people say they barely notice anything, or that it looks fine, I still see it right away.\n\nSo it’s not only that I’m scared people will judge me. It’s more like… to me, it already looks wrong, and then I assume other people must be seeing what I’m seeing. That’s why I came in, because I can’t seem to stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about your face, is it the fact that you think it looks uneven that bothers you, or is it the thought that others might notice and judge you?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the fact that I think it looks uneven. The judging part is there, but it comes after, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, the reason I came in is because ever since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work — in event photos, or just being seen more — I can’t stop looking at this one part of my face in pictures. It’s like my eyes go straight there before I even have a chance to look at the rest of the photo. And then I keep zooming in or staring at it, trying to decide if it’s really that uneven or if maybe it’s just the lighting or the angle, but it still looks wrong to me.\n\nAnd people will say, “I barely see what you mean,” or “You look fine,” but that doesn’t really settle it in my head, because I’m still seeing it so clearly. So yes, I worry other people might notice and judge me, especially because of work, but the part that bothers me first is that I genuinely feel like it looks uneven.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about your face, is it the possibility of being judged by others that's driving your concern, or is it the fact that you genuinely believe there's something wrong with your appearance?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I genuinely believe something looks wrong, honestly. I know the being judged part is wrapped up in it, especially because of work — like, I’m more in front of people now, there are event photos, people taking pictures, sometimes posting them, and I can’t really avoid seeing my face as much as I used to.\n\nBut the thing that gets me stuck is this one uneven-looking part of my face. I’ll see a photo and I don’t even really notice the rest of it first. I go straight to that area, and then I’ll keep staring at it, like I’m trying to figure out if it’s actually uneven or if maybe it’s just the angle or lighting. And it doesn’t really matter if everyone else says they barely see anything, because I still see it right away.\n\nSo yes, I do worry people might judge me, but it’s not like that’s the whole reason. It’s more like I already feel convinced it looks off, and then I think, well, if I can see it this clearly, other people probably can too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

My eye goes straight to it every time. I’ll try to look at the whole photo like a normal person, but I end up focusing on that one area and it feels like it ruins the whole thing.\n\nAnd what makes it confusing is that other people will say they barely see anything, or they’ll tell me it looks fine, but that doesn’t really match what I’m seeing. So it’s not only, “I’m scared they’ll judge me.” It’s more like I’m looking at it and thinking, “No, this really looks uneven,” and then I get scared other people are thinking the same thing.\n\nDoctor: When you're worrying about your face, is it more about the unevenness itself or the possibility that others might see it and think less of you?\n\nPatient: It’s more about the unevenness itself, I think. Like, the other-people part is there, obviously, because I’m in front of people more at work now and there are photos from events and things where I know people can see my face. But it starts with me looking at it and feeling like that one part just looks wrong or uneven.\n\nI’ll see a photo and I can’t really take in the whole picture. My eyes go straight to that spot, and then I’ll keep staring at it, like I’m trying to figure out if it’s as bad as it looks or if it’s just the angle. And even when people tell me they barely see anything, it doesn’t really help much, because I still see it. It’s like I can’t unsee it once I notice it.\n\nSo I guess I do worry that other people might notice and think something, but the main thing is that to me it already looks noticeable. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more the second one — that I genuinely think something looks wrong. I mean, I do think about being judged, especially now that I’m more visible at work and there are photos from events and things like that, but that’s not really where it starts for me.

It starts when I see my face in a picture, and I immediately lock onto this one part that looks uneven to me. I can’t just glance at the photo and move on like other people seem to. I’ll stare at that area, and then I’ll keep checking it, like I’m trying to prove to myself whether it’s really as noticeable as it looks to me. And even when people say they barely see anything, or they tell me it looks normal, I don’t really feel reassured because I still see it.

So the fear of other people noticing is there, but it’s more like… because I already see it so clearly, I assume other people must be seeing it too. That’s why it’s been bothering me enough to come in.
